  Engineering Manager Take-Home                                                 
  Problem: Job Management Dashboard                                             
  Introduction                                                                  
  This take-home assignment evaluates your skills in building a full stack      
  prototype with AI tools.                                                      
  We're looking for your ability to produce clean, well-structured code,        
  understand how to use                                                         
  generative AI, and then deploy that application.                              
  While we do not expect engineering managers to write code day-to-day, the     
  landscape of AI                                                               
  agent software development capability is advancing quickly. At Rescale we     
  want to ensure                                                                
  leadership has a good understanding of the power and gotchas of such tools.   
  The problem should be completable within a few hours. Please focus on         
  delivering a working                                                          
  solution that meets the core requirements.                                    
  Problem Statement: Simple Job Management Dashboard                            
  Rescale helps customers manage complex computational jobs. For this           
  assignment, you will                                                          
  build a simplified "Job Management Dashboard" where users can view, create,   
  and manage the                                                                
  status of computational jobs.                                                 
  Core Requirements                                                             
  1. Backend (Django & PostgreSQL)                                              
  ○                                                                             
  Create a Django project with a single application.                            
  ○                                                                             
  Define a Job model with the following fields:                                 
  ■                                                                             
  id: Primary key (automatically generated).                                    
  ■                                                                             
  name: A string representing the job's name (e.g.,                             
  "Fluid Dynamics                                                               
  Simulation"                                                                   
  ,                                                                             
  "ML Model Training").                                                         
  ■ created_at: Datetime field, automatically set on creation.                  
  ■ updated_at: Datetime field, automatically updated on each save.             
  ○                                                                             
  Define a JobStatus model with the following fields:                           
  ■                                                                             
  id: Primary key (automatically generated).                                    
  ■ job: A foreign key linking to the Job model.                                
  ■ status_type: A choice field with at least four distinct states (e.g.,       
  PENDING, RUNNING, COMPLETED, FAILED).                                         
  ■ timestamp: Datetime field, captures when the status was recorded.           
  ○                                                                             
  Implement a RESTful API with the following endpoints:                         
  ■ GET /api/jobs/: List all jobs. This endpoint should include the             
  current status for each job (i.e., the status_type from the latest            
  JobStatus entry for that job).                                                
  ■                                                                             
  POST /api/jobs/: Create a new job. Upon creation, an initial                  
  JobStatus entry (e.g., PENDING) should be automatically created for           
  this job.                                                                     
  ■                                                                             
  PATCH /api/jobs/<id>/: Update a job. This action should involve               
  creating a new JobStatus entry for the job with the updated                   
  status_type.                                                                  
  ■                                                                             
  DELETE /api/jobs/<id>/: Delete a specific job. Ensure that all                
  associated JobStatus entries are also deleted.                                
  ○                                                                             
  Configure the Django application to connect to a PostgreSQL database.         
  2. Frontend (React & TypeScript)                                              
  ○                                                                             
  Create a React application (e.g., using Create React App or Vite).            
  ○                                                                             
  Display a list of all jobs fetched from your Django backend. Each job in the  
  list                                                                          
  should show its name and current status.                                      
  ○                                                                             
  Provide a form or input field to allow users to create a new job by entering  
  a                                                                             
  name.                                                                         
  ○                                                                             
  For each job in the list, provide a mechanism (e.g., a dropdown, buttons, or  
  a                                                                             
  modal) to update its status to any of the defined states. This action should  
  trigger                                                                       
  the creation of a new JobStatus entry on the backend.                         
  ○                                                                             
  For each job, provide a button to delete it.                                  
  ○                                                                             
  Implement basic error handling for API calls (e.g., display a simple message  
  if                                                                            
  fetching or updating fails).                                                  
  ○                                                                             
  Implement simple client-side validation for the new job form (e.g., name      
  cannot                                                                        
  be empty).                                                                    
  ○                                                                             
  Ensure the UI updates dynamically after creating, updating, or deleting jobs. 
  ○                                                                             
  Style the application using a method of your choice to make the UI            
  presentable.                                                                  
  3. Testing                                                                    
  ○                                                                             
  Include at least one End-to-End (E2E) test using Playwright. This test should 
  cover a critical user flow, such as:                                          
  ■ Creating a new job and verifying it appears in the list with the correct    
  initial status.                                                               
  ■ Updating a job's status to a different available status and verifying the   
  change.                                                                       
  4. Deployment & Setup                                                         
  ○                                                                             
  Provide Dockerfiles for both your Django backend and your React frontend      
  (or a single Dockerfile for the backend and instructions for serving the      
  React                                                                         
  build).                                                                       
  ○                                                                             
  Provide a docker-compose.yml file that orchestrates your Django backend,      
  PostgreSQL database, and React frontend, allowing for easy setup and running  
  of the entire application with a single command.                              
  ○                                                                             
  Include a Makefile with the following commands:                               
  ■                                                                             
  make build: Builds the Docker images.                                         
  ■                                                                             
  make up: Starts the entire application stack using Docker Compose.            
  ■                                                                             
  make test: Runs your Playwright E2E tests.                                    
  ■                                                                             
  make stop: Stops the running Docker containers.                               
  ■                                                                             
  make clean: Removes Docker volumes/networks if necessary for a                
  clean slate.                                                                  
  5. Performance Considerations                                                 
  ○                                                                             
  While building the application, consider web performance optimization.        
  Imagine a                                                                     
  scenario where there could be millions of jobs in the database.               
  ○                                                                             
  Address how you would efficiently fetch and display this large dataset in the 
  frontend.                                                                     
  ○                                                                             
  Briefly describe any performance considerations you made or optimizations you 
  implemented in your README.md file                                            
  6. Running a Job                                                              
  ○                                                                             
  For this exercise, you are not required to actually build the job scheduling  
  system.                                                                       
  AI Usage Guidelines

⏺ Both planning docs are written (docs/SPEC.md, docs/PLAN.md). Plan mode is now
  on, so I'll hold off creating the rest until we settle the open questions —
  here's my understanding first.

  1. Models & API, as I read them

  JobStatus is an append-only event log, not a mutable field. The prompt's PATCH
  wording — "creating a new JobStatus entry" — makes that explicit.
  Job.current_status is therefore a projection of that log. Everything else
  follows from that framing.

  - Job: id, name, created_at, updated_at + denormalized current_status,
  current_status_at
  - JobStatus: id, job (FK CASCADE), status_type (4 TextChoices), timestamp
  - GET /api/jobs/ cursor-paginated with current_status inline · POST creates
  job + initial PENDING in one transaction · PATCH appends an event (also
  handles rename) · DELETE cascades · plus GET /api/health/ so make test can
  wait for readiness instead of racing Postgres

  2. Slices

  11 vertical slices, each with one scoped Playwright spec. Slices 0–4 (health →
  list → create → patch → delete) are backend-only but still tested through
  Playwright's request fixture — same runner, same make test, no second
  framework. Slices 5–10 layer the UI on: list → create form → status update 
  (the prompt's required critical flow) → delete → fault-injected error handling
  → pagination at scale. Slice 11 is README + a clean-machine make test rerun.
  Details in docs/PLAN.md.

  3. On your two questions — I agree, with one refinement each

  Millions of jobs: your reading is right, and I'd state it in the README as an
  explicit assumption: the dataset is large, a single user's viewport is not.
  The real job is making every query cost proportional to the page, not the
  table. Concretely that means cursor over offset pagination — OFFSET 500000
  makes Postgres walk and discard half a million rows, and DRF's page-number
  paginator additionally fires a COUNT(*) on every request, which is a full
  index scan per page load. Keyset pagination has neither problem.

  Current status: agreed on denormalizing onto Job — the deciding factor is
  ?status=RUNNING, which a derive-on-read subquery cannot serve without
  computing the latest status for every job in the table. One refinement:
  compare the incoming event against current_status_at, not updated_at.
  updated_at bumps on every save, so a rename would make a subsequent legitimate
  status event look stale and get silently dropped.

  4. Things the prompt leaves open

  Flagging the ones that don't need a decision from you — I'll note them in the
  README rather than build for them: no auth/multi-tenancy (the prompt never
  mentions users, so one shared job list); no state-machine constraints (prompt
  says update to any defined state, so COMPLETED → RUNNING is allowed); repeat 
  PATCH of the same status still appends an event, since it's a log of
  observations, not a diff.

  One gotcha worth surfacing now: the evaluation environment may only assume
  DockerHub access, but the standard Playwright image lives on
  mcr.microsoft.com, not DockerHub. I'll build the e2e image from
  node:22-bookworm instead. Getting that wrong fails make test on their machine,
  which per the prompt ends the evaluation.

  Four decisions I'd like from you:

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Where should status `timestamp` come from? This decides whether the 
     monotonic guard is load-bearing or just insurance. → Server-stamped only 
     (Recommended)
     · Expose the status history (`GET /api/jobs/<id>/statuses/`)? The prompt 
     doesn't ask for it, but it's the only way to *prove* the log is append-only
     and that DELETE cascaded. → Endpoint + UI timeline (Recommended)
     · How should the frontend be served in Docker? → nginx static build + /api 
     proxy (Recommended)
     · What should `make test` run? The prompt specifies Playwright E2E, but 
     backend unit tests are cheap signal. → Playwright only, backend tests 
     separate (Recommended)

     │                                                                         │
     │ - JobStatus is an append-only event log; Job.current_status is a        │
     │ denormalized projection of it.                                          │
     │ Justified by ?status=RUNNING — a derive-on-read subquery would have to  │
     │ compute the latest status for                                           │
     │ every row in the table before filtering, which can't use an index.      │
     │ - Guard the projection on current_status_at, not updated_at —           │

     │ a rename would make a later legitimate status event look stale and get  │
     │ dropped.                                                                │
     │ - Server-stamped timestamps. The guard still ships (one comparison,     │
     │ makes writes idempotent under                                           │
     │ replay) but is inert today.                                             │
     │ - Status history exposed: GET /api/jobs/<id>/statuses/ + an expandable  │
     │ per-row timeline in the UI.                                             │
     │ Lets the tests assert the log is genuinely append-only and that DELETE  │
     │ cascaded.                                                               │

     │ - make test = Playwright only (exactly what the prompt specifies); make │

     │ assumptions                                                             │
     │ docs/TIME_LOG.md + scripts/timelog.sh                                   │
     │                                                                         │
     │ DockerHub constraint: the e2e image must not use                        │
     │ mcr.microsoft.com/playwright — it isn't on                              │
     │ DockerHub, and the prompt only guarantees DockerHub access. Build from  │
     │ node:22-bookworm. Pin every                                             │
     │ base image tag; verify the build works on linux/amd64 as well as Apple  │
     │ Silicon.                                                                │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Core implementation notes                                               │
     │                                                                         │
     │ services.record_status(job, status_type) is the only function that      │
     │ writes current_status. It runs                                          │
     │ in transaction.atomic() with select_for_update() on the job row,        │
     │ appends the JobStatus, then applies                                     │
     │ the projection if new.timestamp >= job.current_status_at. POST and      │
     │ PATCH both route through it, so                                         │
     │ there is exactly one code path that can create drift between the log    │
     │ and the projection.                                                     │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Indexes — JobStatus(job_id, timestamp DESC, id DESC), Job(created_at    │
     │ DESC, id DESC),                                                         │
     │ Job(current_status, created_at DESC, id DESC). id DESC as tiebreaker    │
     │ everywhere so ordering is total;                                        │
     │ without it, rows sharing a timestamp can be skipped or repeated across  │
     │ keyset pages.                                                           │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Pagination — DRF CursorPagination, ordering = ("-created_at", "-id"),   │
     │ default page size 25. No                                                │
     │ COUNT(*) on the hot path.                                               │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Frontend — TanStack Query for server state (caching, invalidation,      │
     │ error/loading states are most of                                        │
     │ what this app does). Optimistic status updates with rollback on failure │
     │ — slice 9 tests the rollback.                                           │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Build order                                                             │
     │                                                                         │
     │ Each slice is independently green before the next begins; commit per    │
     │ slice. Backend-only slices are still                                    │
     │ verified through Playwright's request fixture, so there's one runner    │
     │ and one make test throughout.                                           │

⏺ Fair on all four — especially #9. Error handling shouldn't be a slice; it's a
  property of every slice. Rewriting.

⏺ Updated plan
  ⎿  /plan to preview

⏺ Rewritten. What changed:

  Validation is now explicit per slice, on three tiers: T1 lightweight (make 
  test-spec SPEC=…, one spec against the running stack, seconds) after every
  change inside a slice; T2 full suite at the close of every slice; T3 cold gate
  (make clean && make build && make test from pruned Docker + amd64 check) at
  slices 0, 4, 7, 11 — plus any slice that touches Docker, compose, the
  Makefile, or a dependency, since that's the class of change that passes warm
  and breaks cold.

  You're right about #9. Error handling is now built into each slice's
  definition of done — uniform DRF error shape in slice 1, serializer validation
  in 2, typed client + ErrorBanner in 5, form validation in 6, optimistic
  rollback in 7. What survives at position 9 is a fault-injection sweep: a spec
  that systematically proves the handling built in 1–8 holds under 500s, aborts,
  and slow responses. Building the handling early and verifying it
  systematically later is the right split; deferring the handling itself would
  have meant retrofitting it into eight slices.

  Two mermaid diagrams: container topology (showing why the nginx /api proxy
  gives a single origin and kills CORS), and a sequence diagram of the status
  write — the one piece of non-obvious logic, where the log is always appended
  but the projection update is conditional.

  Test plan matrix: 34 cases, 15 negative, grouped A–F (create / update / delete
  / list / scale / infra), each with action, expected behaviour, and the
  specific validation. Sign-off gate sits after slice 0 — the skeleton ships
  first so the plan is reviewed against something real, then you approve before
  feature work starts. A few worth your eye: B8 (rename must not append a status
  event), B9 (concurrent PATCHes — no lost update), C4 (failed delete restores
  the row), E2 (no duplicate or skipped ids across the cursor walk).

  ⎿  Plan saved to: ~/.claude/plans/nifty-shimmying-map.md · /plan to edit
     Job Management Dashboard — Implementation Plan

     Context

     Take-home for an EM role at Rescale: a full-stack Django + PostgreSQL +
     React/TS "Job Management
     Dashboard", containerized, with Playwright E2E and a make test gate. The
     repo is currently empty
     (one PDF in docs/), so this is greenfield.

     Two things shape the build beyond the literal requirements:

     1. The evaluation has a hard gate. The graders run make test on a clean
     machine with only
     make/docker/docker-compose/bash and DockerHub access. If that fails they
     stop reading. So the
     containerized test pipeline gets built first, before any feature code.
     2. The prompt asks for judgment, not just features — a performance writeup
     for a hypothetical
     millions-of-jobs dataset, and a prompt-engineering writeup. Design
     decisions need to be legible and
     defensible in a follow-up conversation, since the stated expectation is
     being able to explain
     AI-generated code as your own.

     Already written this session: docs/SPEC.md (models, API, status-derivation
     rationale, scale analysis)
     and docs/PLAN.md (slice detail).

     Decisions settled with the user

     - JobStatus is an append-only event log; Job.current_status is a
     denormalized projection of it.
     Justified by ?status=RUNNING — a derive-on-read subquery must compute the
     latest status for every row
     in the table before it can filter, which cannot use an index.
     - Guard the projection on current_status_at, not updated_at — updated_at
     moves on every save, so
     a rename would make a later legitimate status event look stale and get
     silently dropped.
     - Server-stamped timestamps. The guard still ships (one comparison, makes
     writes idempotent under
     replay) but is inert today.
     - Status history exposed: GET /api/jobs/<id>/statuses/ + an expandable
     per-row timeline. Lets tests
     assert the log is genuinely append-only and that DELETE cascaded.
     - nginx serves the static Vite build and reverse-proxies /api → no CORS, no
     build-time API URL.
     - make test = Playwright only (exactly what the prompt specifies); make 
     test-backend /
     make test-all run pytest-django separately, so an unrelated unit test can
     never fail the gate.
     - Out of scope, documented in the README rather than built: no
     auth/multi-tenancy, no status state machine
     (any transition allowed), repeat PATCH of the same status still appends an
     event.

     ---
     Architecture

     mermaid
     flowchart LR
         subgraph host["docker compose"]
             direction LR
             fe["<b>frontend</b><br/>nginx:alpine<br/>static Vite build<br/>+
     /api reverse proxy<br/>:8080"]
             be["<b>backend</b><br/>gunicorn + Django<br/>DRF
     ViewSet<br/>:8000"]
             db[("<b>db</b><br/>postgres:16-alpine<br/>:5432")]
             e2e["<b>e2e</b><br/>node:22-bookworm<br/>+ chromium<br/><i>run-once
     profile</i>"]
         end
         user(["Browser"]) -->|":8080"| fe
         fe -->|"/api/* proxy"| be
         be -->|"psycopg"| db
         e2e -.->|"UI specs"| fe
         e2e -.->|"API specs<br/>(request fixture)"| fe

         classDef svc fill:#1e293b,stroke:#475569,color:#e2e8f0
         class fe,be,db,e2e svc

     nginx proxying /api means the browser and Playwright both see a single 
     origin — no CORS config, and
     no API base URL baked in at build time. The e2e container drives everything
     through that same origin, so
     the tests exercise the real proxy path rather than a shortcut.

     The status write is the one piece of non-obvious logic, so it gets its own
     diagram:

     mermaid
     sequenceDiagram
         autonumber
         participant UI as React (optimistic)
         participant API as DRF JobViewSet
         participant SVC as services.record_status()
         participant DB as Postgres

         UI->>UI: badge → RUNNING immediately
         UI->>API: PATCH /api/jobs/12/ {status:"RUNNING"}
         API->>SVC: record_status(job, RUNNING)

         rect rgb(30,41,59)
         note over SVC,DB: transaction.atomic()
         SVC->>DB: SELECT … FOR UPDATE (job row lock)
         SVC->>DB: INSERT JobStatus(job, RUNNING, now())
         alt new.timestamp >= job.current_status_at
             SVC->>DB: UPDATE job SET current_status, current_status_at
         else stale / replayed event
             SVC-->>SVC: append only, projection untouched
         end
         end

         API-->>UI: 200 {current_status:"RUNNING", …}
         UI->>UI: reconcile cache
         Note over UI: on 4xx/5xx → roll back badge,<br/>surface ErrorBanner

     The log is always appended; only the projection is conditional. That's what
     makes the write safe to
     replay and what keeps the log authoritative if the projection ever needs
     rebuilding.

     ---
     Files to create

     Makefile                        build up test test-spec test-backend
     test-all stop clean seed logs time
     docker-compose.yml              db · backend · frontend · e2e (run-once
     profile)
     README.md                       setup · architecture · perf writeup ·
     prompt-engineering writeup · time
     backend/Dockerfile              python:3.12-slim + gunicorn
     backend/config/settings.py      env-driven; DATABASE_URL; DRF cursor
     pagination default
     backend/jobs/models.py          Job, JobStatus, StatusType + composite
     indexes
     backend/jobs/serializers.py     JobSerializer (write-only `status`),
     JobStatusSerializer
     backend/jobs/views.py           JobViewSet, statuses action, health
     backend/jobs/services.py        record_status() — the ONLY writer of the
     projection
     backend/jobs/management/commands/seed_jobs.py
     backend/jobs/tests/             pytest-django
     frontend/Dockerfile             node build → nginx:alpine
     frontend/nginx.conf             static + /api proxy to backend
     frontend/src/api/jobs.ts        single typed client — base URL + error
     normalization live here only
     frontend/src/components/        JobList · JobRow · StatusBadge ·
     StatusSelect · CreateJobForm ·
                                     StatusTimeline · ErrorBanner
     e2e/Dockerfile                  node:22-bookworm + `npx playwright install
     --with-deps chromium`
     e2e/*.spec.ts                   one spec per slice
     docs/TEST_PLAN.md               the matrix below, extracted for sign-off
     docs/OPEN_QUESTIONS.md          settled decisions + documented assumptions
     docs/TIME_LOG.md + scripts/timelog.sh

     DockerHub constraint: the e2e image must not use
     mcr.microsoft.com/playwright — it isn't on
     DockerHub, and the prompt only guarantees DockerHub access. Build from
     node:22-bookworm. Pin every base
     image tag; verify linux/amd64 as well as Apple Silicon.

     Core implementation notes

     services.record_status(job, status_type) is the only function that writes
     current_status. It runs in
     transaction.atomic() with select_for_update() on the job row, appends the
     JobStatus, then applies the
     projection per the guard. POST and PATCH both route through it, so exactly
     one code path can create
     drift between log and projection.

     Indexes — JobStatus(job_id, timestamp DESC, id DESC), Job(created_at DESC, 
     id DESC),
     Job(current_status, created_at DESC, id DESC). id DESC as tiebreaker
     everywhere so ordering is total;
     without it, rows sharing a timestamp can be skipped or repeated across
     keyset pages.

     Pagination — DRF CursorPagination, ordering = ("-created_at", "-id"), page
     size 25. No COUNT(*)
     on the hot path.

     Error handling is cross-cutting, not a phase. Per your note — it is built
     into each slice's definition
     of done, never retrofitted:

     ┌───────────────────────────────┬──────────┬─────────────────────────────┐
     │             Layer             │ Built in │        What it does         │
     │                               │   slice  │                             │
     ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ DRF exception handler →       │ 1        │ every 4xx/5xx has one       │
     │ uniform {detail, errors}      │          │ predictable shape           │
     ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ Model + serializer validation │ 2        │ rejects blank/oversized     │
     │                               │          │ names at the boundary       │
     ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ Typed client normalizes       │          │ one ApiError type, one      │
     │ network + HTTP + parse        │ 5        │ place to change             │
     │ failures                      │          │                             │
     ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ ErrorBanner + query error     │ 5        │ no blank screens, ever      │
     │ states                        │          │                             │
     ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ Client-side form validation   │ 6        │ blocks the request before   │
     │                               │          │ it's sent                   │
     ├───────────────────────────────┼──────────┼─────────────────────────────┤
     │ Optimistic mutation +         │ 7        │ UI never lies about         │
     │ rollback on failure           │          │ persisted state             │
     └───────────────────────────────┴──────────┴─────────────────────────────┘

     The old "slice 9 = error handling" is gone. What remains at that position
     is a fault-injection sweep —
     a dedicated spec that systematically proves the handling built in slices
     1–8 actually holds under 500s,
     aborts, and slow responses. Building the handling early and verifying it 
     systematically later is the
     right split; deferring the handling itself would have meant retrofitting it
     into eight slices.

     ---
     Validation tiers

     Three tiers, so the inner loop stays fast and the expensive gate still runs
     often enough to catch drift.

     Tier: T1 — lightweight
     Command: make test-spec SPEC=03-update-job-api (one spec against the
     already-running stack, no rebuild) · make test-backend during backend
     slices
     Cost: seconds
     When: after every meaningful change, inside a slice
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Tier: T2 — suite
     Command: make test (rebuild + full Playwright suite, stack already
     provisioned)
     Cost: ~1–2 min
     When: at the close of every slice — this is what catches cross-slice
     regressions
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     Tier: T3 — cold gate
     Command: make clean && make build && make test from a pruned Docker +
     --platform linux/amd64 build check
     Cost: several min
     When: slices 0, 4, 7, 11 only

     T3 is deliberately rare — it's the evaluator's exact path, and it's slow.
     It runs at slice 0 (proves the
     gate before any features exist), slice 4 (backend complete), slice 7
     (required critical flow green), and
     slice 11 (final). Any slice that touches Docker, compose, the Makefile, or
     a dependency also triggers
     T3, regardless of position — that's the class of change that breaks cold
     builds while passing warm ones.

     ---
     Build order

     Each slice is independently green before the next begins; commit per slice.
     Backend-only slices are verified through Playwright's request fixture — one
     runner, one make test.

     ┌─────┬───────────────────────────┬─────────────────────┬─────────────────┐
     │  #  │           Slice           │        Spec         │ Validation at   │
     │     │                           │                     │      close      │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │     │ Compose, Dockerfiles,     │                     │ T3 — cold gate  │
     │ 0   │ Makefile, /api/health/,   │ 00-smoke            │ must pass       │
     │     │ React shell               │                     │ before any      │
     │     │                           │                     │ feature code    │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │     │ Models, migrations,       │                     │                 │
     │ 1   │ indexes, GET /api/jobs/   │ 01-jobs-list-api    │ T1 per change → │
     │     │ cursor pagination, error  │                     │  T2             │
     │     │ handler                   │                     │                 │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │ 2   │ POST + auto-PENDING,      │ 02-create-job-api   │ T1 → T2         │
     │     │ atomic, name validation   │                     │                 │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │     │ PATCH appends event,      │                     │ T1 → T2 +       │
     │ 3   │ projection guard,         │ 03-update-job-api   │ concurrency     │
     │     │ select_for_update         │                     │ case B9         │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │ 4   │ DELETE + cascade, GET     │ 04-delete-job-api   │ T3 — backend    │
     │     │ …/statuses/               │                     │ complete        │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │     │ UI list, badges, typed    │                     │                 │
     │ 5   │ client, ErrorBanner,      │ 05-job-list-ui      │ T1 → T2         │
     │     │ loading/empty, styling    │                     │                 │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │ 6   │ Create form + client-side │ 06-create-job-ui    │ T1 → T2         │
     │     │  validation               │                     │                 │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │     │ Status update — the       │                     │ T3 — the flow   │
     │ 7   │ prompt's required         │ 07-update-status-ui │ they will look  │
     │     │ critical flow +           │                     │ for first       │
     │     │ optimistic rollback       │                     │                 │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │ 8   │ Delete (in-app confirm,   │ 08-delete-job-ui    │ T1 → T2         │
     │     │ never window.confirm)     │                     │                 │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │     │ Fault-injection sweep     │                     │                 │
     │ 9   │ (page.route()): 500 per   │ 09-fault-injection  │ T2              │
     │     │ verb, abort, slow,        │                     │                 │
     │     │ rollback, recovery        │                     │                 │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │     │ Scale: load-more/infinite │                     │                 │
     │ 10  │  scroll, status filter,   │ 10-pagination-scale │ T2 + latency    │
     │     │ debounced search, seeded  │                     │ measurement     │
     │     │ 250k                      │                     │                 │
     ├─────┼───────────────────────────┼─────────────────────┼─────────────────┤
     │     │ README, both writeups,    │                     │ T3 ×2 — warm,   │
     │ 11  │ final tidy                │ full suite          │ then fully      │
     │     │                           │                     │ pruned          │
     └─────┴───────────────────────────┴─────────────────────┴─────────────────┘

     Slice 0 first is deliberate: build the pipe before the water. If time runs
     short the fallback is a smaller
     UI, never a broken make test.

     Test isolation — E2E runs against real Postgres, so no spec may assume an
     empty DB. Every spec creates
     fixtures under a run-unique prefix (e2e-<uuid>-…), scopes assertions to
     those rows, and cleans up. The
     suite is re-runnable without make clean — itself asserted by case F3.

     ---
     Test plan — sign-off required before slice 1

     Extracted to docs/TEST_PLAN.md as the first execution step. Slice 0 ships, 
     then this gets your explicit
     sign-off before feature work starts. + = positive case, − = negative case.

     A · Create job

     ID: A1
     ±: +
     Action: Submit valid name
     Expected behaviour: 201; current_status=PENDING; current_status_at set
     Validation: row appears at top without refresh; log has exactly 1 entry;
     input
     clears
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: A2
     ±: −
     Action: Submit empty name
     Expected behaviour: inline validation message
     Validation: zero network requests fired; no row created
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: A3
     ±: −
     Action: Submit " "
     Expected behaviour: rejected as blank (trimmed)
     Validation: same as A2
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: A4
     ±: −
     Action: Name > 200 chars
     Expected behaviour: rejected, message visible
     Validation: no row created; API returns 400 if forced
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: A5
     ±: −
     Action: POST returns 500
     Expected behaviour: error banner shown
     Validation: typed name preserved; no phantom row after reload
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: A6
     ±: +
     Action: Name with unicode/emoji/quotes/<script>
     Expected behaviour: persists verbatim
     Validation: renders correctly, escaped — no injection
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: A7
     ±: +
     Action: Two jobs with identical names
     Expected behaviour: both created
     Validation: independently addressable; no uniqueness constraint

     B · Update status

     ID: B1
     ±: +
     Action: PENDING → RUNNING
     Expected behaviour: badge updates immediately
     Validation: persists across reload; log has 2 entries, PENDING still
     present
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: B2
     ±: +
     Action: Reach all four states
     Expected behaviour: each selectable and persisted
     Validation: badge label + colour correct per state
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: B3
     ±: +
     Action: COMPLETED → RUNNING (backwards)
     Expected behaviour: allowed
     Validation: documents the no-state-machine decision; log grows
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: B4
     ±: +
     Action: Same status twice
     Expected behaviour: new event appended
     Validation: log grows; current_status unchanged; current_status_at advances
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: B5
     ±: −
     Action: status: "NOT_A_STATUS" via API
     Expected behaviour: 400
     Validation: log unchanged; projection unchanged
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: B6
     ±: −
     Action: PATCH nonexistent id
     Expected behaviour: 404
     Validation: no side effects
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: B7
     ±: −
     Action: PATCH returns 500
     Expected behaviour: optimistic badge rolls back to prior value
     Validation: error shown; log unchanged after reload
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: B8
     ±: +
     Action: Rename only, no status key
     Expected behaviour: name changes
     Validation: no new status event appended — log length unchanged
     ────────────────────────────────────────
     ID: B9
     ±: +
     Action: Two concurrent PATCHes
     Expected behaviour: both events logged
     Validation: current_status = the later one; no lost update (row lock holds)

     C · Delete

     ┌─────┬─────┬──────────────────────┬────────────────┬─────────────────────┐
     │ ID  │  ±  │        Action        │   Expected     │     Validation      │

     │     │     │ id                   │                │                     │

     one negative case.

     ---
     Time tracker

     scripts/timelog.sh start|stop|status|report appending to docs/TIME_LOG.md —
     a markdown table of
     start · stop · duration · slice · notes, computed total, and an ACTIVE
     marker while a session is open.
     make time prints the report. The total feeds the README's required "time
     spent" line, so it needs to be
     accurate from slice 0 rather than reconstructed at the end.

     Deliverable the user owns

     The prompt requires a screen recording or transcript link of the AI
     interactions. This session is part
     of that record — worth preserving before the context is cleared.

  clock first so the tracker is honest from this point.

⏺ Update(docs/SPEC.md)
  ⎿  Added 91 lines
        6  
        7  ---
        8  
        9 +## 0. Architecture                                            
       10 +                                                              
       11 +```mermaid                                                    
       12 +flowchart LR                                                  
       13 +    subgraph host["docker compose"]                           
       14 +        direction LR                                          
       15 +        fe["<b>frontend</b><br/>nginx:alpine<br/>static Vite b
          +uild<br/>+ /api reverse proxy<br/>:80"]                       
       16 +        be["<b>backend</b><br/>gunicorn + Django<br/>DRF ViewS
          +et<br/>:8000"]                                                
       17 +        db[("<b>db</b><br/>postgres:16-alpine<br/>:5432")]    
       18 +        e2e["<b>e2e</b><br/>node:22-bookworm<br/>+ chromium<br
          +/><i>run-once profile</i>"]                                   
       19 +    end                                                       
       20 +    user(["Browser<br/>localhost:8080"]) --> fe               
       21 +    fe -->|"/api/* proxy"| be                                 
       22 +    be -->|"psycopg"| db                                      
       23 +    e2e -.->|"UI specs"| fe                                   
       24 +    e2e -.->|"API specs<br/>(request fixture)"| fe            
       25 +                                                              
       26 +    classDef svc fill:#1e293b,stroke:#475569,color:#e2e8f0    
       27 +    class fe,be,db,e2e svc                                    
       28 +```                                                           
       29 +                                                              
       30 +nginx proxying `/api` gives the browser and Playwright a **sin
          +gle origin** — no CORS configuration, and no                  
       31 +API base URL baked in at build time, so the same image runs an
          +ywhere. The e2e container drives everything                   
       32 +through that same origin, meaning the tests exercise the real 
          +request path rather than a shortcut around                    
       33 +it.                                                           
       34 +                                                              
       35 +### Data model                                                
       36 +                                                              
       37 +```mermaid                                                    
       38 +erDiagram                                                     
       39 +    JOB ||--o{ JOB_STATUS : "has many (CASCADE)"              
       40 +                                                              
       41 +    JOB {                                                     
       42 +        bigint   id PK                                        
       43 +        varchar  name "max 200, non-blank"                    
       44 +        datetime created_at "auto_now_add"                    
       45 +        datetime updated_at "auto_now"                        
       46 +        varchar  current_status "PROJECTION — written only by 
          +record_status()"                                              
       47 +        datetime current_status_at "timestamp of the event beh
          +ind current_status"                                           
       48 +    }                                                         
       49 +                                                              
       50 +    JOB_STATUS {                                              
       51 +        bigint   id PK                                        
       52 +        bigint   job_id FK                                    
       53 +        varchar  status_type "PENDING | RUNNING | COMPLETED | 
          +FAILED"                                                       
       54 +        datetime timestamp "server-stamped"                   
       55 +    }                                                         
       56 +```                                                           
       57 +                                                              
       58 +`JOB_STATUS` is the **source of truth** — an append-only log o
          +f observations, never updated, never deleted                  
       59 +except by cascade. The two `current_*` columns on `JOB` are a 
          +**projection** of that log, kept in the same                  
       60 +transaction as the event that produces them. §3 explains why t
          +he projection exists and what keeps it                        
       61 +honest.                                                       
       62 +                                                              
       63 +### The status write                                          
       64 +                                                              
       65 +The one piece of non-obvious control flow:                    
       66 +                                                              
       67 +```mermaid                                                    
       68 +sequenceDiagram                                               
       69 +    autonumber                                                
       70 +    participant UI as React (optimistic)                      
       71 +    participant API as DRF JobViewSet                         
       72 +    participant SVC as services.record_status()               
       73 +    participant DB as Postgres                                
       74 +                                                              
       75 +    UI->>UI: badge → RUNNING immediately                      
       76 +    UI->>API: PATCH /api/jobs/12/ {status:"RUNNING"}          
       77 +    API->>SVC: record_status(job, RUNNING)                    
       78 +                                                              
       79 +    rect rgb(30,41,59)                                        
       80 +    note over SVC,DB: transaction.atomic()                    
       81 +    SVC->>DB: SELECT … FOR UPDATE (job row lock)              
       82 +    SVC->>DB: INSERT JobStatus(job, RUNNING, now())           
       83 +    alt new.timestamp >= job.current_status_at                
       84 +        SVC->>DB: UPDATE job SET current_status, current_statu
          +s_at                                                          
       85 +    else stale / replayed event                               
       86 +        SVC-->>SVC: append only, projection untouched         
       87 +    end                                                       
       88 +    end                                                       
       89 +                                                              
       90 +    API-->>UI: 200 {current_status:"RUNNING", …}              
       91 +    UI->>UI: reconcile cache                                  
       92 +    Note over UI: on 4xx/5xx → roll back badge,<br/>surface Er
          +rorBanner                                                     
       93 +```                                                           
       94 +                                                              
       95 +The event is appended **unconditionally**; only the projection
          + is conditional. That is what makes the write                 
       96 +safe to replay, and what keeps the log authoritative if the pr
          +ojection ever has to be rebuilt.                              
       97 +                                                              
       98 +---                                                           
       99 +

      101  
      102  Two entities, one-to-many.

      30 -nginx proxying `/api` gives the browser and Playwright a **sing
         -le origin** — no CORS configuration, and no                    
      31 -API base URL baked in at build time, so the same image runs any
         -where. The e2e container drives everything                     
      32 -through that same origin, meaning the tests exercise the real r

      33 -it.                                                            
      39 +**`e2e` is the Playwright test container** — the E2E suite itse
         +lf, packaged as a service so it runs on the                    
      40 +compose network with no host dependencies beyond Docker. It sit
         +s behind a `test` profile, so `make up`                        
      41 +never starts it; `make test` builds it, runs it once against th
         +e live stack, and it exits with the suite's                    
      42 +status code. That is what makes `make test` self-contained on a
         + machine that has only make, docker, and                       
      43 +bash.                                                          
      44  
      45 +It drives the app through **the same nginx origin the browser u
         +ses** — including the API specs, which go                      
      46 +through the `/api` proxy rather than talking to Django directly
         +. The tests therefore exercise the real                        
      47 +request path, proxy included, instead of a shortcut around it. 
      48 +                                                               
      49 +nginx proxying `/api` is also what gives the browser and Playwr
         +ight a **single origin**: no CORS

         +ime, so the same image runs in any                             
      51 +environment.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ On your question: e2e is the Playwright suite — so the tests were already in
  the diagram, just wearing an unhelpful name. It's e2e/Dockerfile: node +
  Chromium, containing e2e/tests/*.spec.ts. It sits behind a compose test
  profile, so make up never starts it; make test builds it, runs it once against
  the live stack, and it exits with the suite's status code. That packaging is
  what makes make test work on a machine with only make, docker, and bash.

  Both diagrams now split into two subgraphs — "the running application" vs
  "compose profile test — started only by make test, then exits" — with the node
  labelled e2e — Playwright runner and the two dashed edges distinguishing UI
  specs (drives Chromium) from API specs (request fixture). Worth noting the API
  specs deliberately go through the same nginx origin rather than hitting
  Django directly, so they exercise the real proxy path.

  Transcript moved to docs/ai-session-transcript-2026-07-25.txt (160K) and
  committed. It's the AI-usage deliverable, so it belongs with the other docs;
  the README will link it at slice 11.

  (POST /api/jobs/ with the automatic PENDING status).

✻ Cooked for 35s

❯ Are the validatation tiers just ways to run all or a subset of the tests?     

⏺ Not quite — there are two different axes, and they're easy to conflate because
  T3 looks like "T2 again".

  T1 → T2 is a scope axis. T1 runs one spec against the stack that's already up;
  T2 runs the whole suite. That one genuinely is "subset vs all" (plus make 
  test-backend, which is a different suite entirely — pytest, not Playwright).

  T2 → T3 is not a scope axis at all. They run the identical set of tests. What
  changes is the starting state: T3 wipes images, volumes, and the database
  first, then rebuilds from nothing. Same assertions, different thing under test
  — T2 validates the application, T3 validates the build and provision path.

  That distinction matters because T3 catches a class of failure T2 structurally
  cannot:

  - a file that exists in a stale image layer but was never committed to git
  - a migration that only applies cleanly against an already-migrated database
  - a dependency resolving from a warm cache that isn't actually pinned
  - anything that depends on host state rather than repo state

  Two concrete ones from this session: the initial migration got generated
  inside a container and never landed on the host, and entrypoint.sh was missing
  its exec bit locally — the Dockerfile's chmod masked it. Both were invisible
  warm and obvious cold. That's exactly the shape of "works on my machine," and
  it's the shape of failure the evaluation gate is built to catch, since they
  clone fresh and run make test once.

  So the cheap/expensive tradeoff isn't really about test count — T3 costs
  several minutes because of image rebuilds and the Chromium download, not
  because it runs more assertions. Which is why the rule is positional (slices
  0, 4, 7, 11) plus unconditional whenever Docker, compose, the Makefile, or
  dependencies change — that's the change class that passes warm and fails cold.

  Want me to make that explicit in TEST_PLAN.md §2? It currently implies the
  distinction through the commands without stating it outright.

⏺ Committed. Two substantive design questions — let me take them in turn.

  On updated_at: yes, and that's a real gap. The C-group cases assert
  current_status and the log length but never that the job row's timestamps
  moved. That matters because updated_at and current_status_at are supposed to 
  diverge in specific ways, and nothing currently pins that down:

  ┌──────────────────────┬────────────────┬───────────────────┬────────────┐
  │        Action        │ current_status │ current_status_at │ updated_at │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Status change        │ changes        │ advances          │ advances   │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Rename only (C8)     │ unchanged      │ must not move     │ advances   │
  ├──────────────────────┼────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ Stale/replayed event │ unchanged      │ must not move     │ unchanged  │
  └──────────────────────┴────────────────┴───────────────────┴────────────┘

  That middle row is the whole reason the guard compares against
  current_status_at instead of updated_at — so asserting it belongs in the E2E
  cases, not just the backend unit test where it currently lives. I'll add those
  assertions to C1, C4, and C8.

  On the state machine — your instinct is right about the domain and I don't
  want to wave it away. COMPLETED → RUNNING is genuinely meaningless in a real
  scheduler; a re-run is a new attempt, not a backwards edit of the old one.

  The complication is what the dropdown actually is here. The prompt excludes
  building a scheduler, so the status control isn't a user workflow action —
  it's a stand-in for the events a scheduler would emit. Under that reading,
  restricting it removes your only way to reach a state for demo or test
  purposes. And the prompt says the mechanism should update status "to any of
  the defined states," so a hard state machine means an evaluator clicking
  through the UI finds statuses they can't select, which reads as a bug rather
  than a design decision.

  On same-status-twice: agreed it isn't a product feature. But I'd keep a thin
  version of the case rather than drop it, precisely for the reason you named —
  a double-submit or retry race can produce it, and the useful assertion is that
  it's harmless: appends an event, projection unchanged, timestamps advance,
  nothing corrupted. That's robustness, not a feature.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · How should status transitions be constrained? → Permissive, but policy 
     named and explicit (Recommended)
     · What should happen to case C4 (same status applied twice)? → Keep, 
     reframed as race robustness (Recommended)

⏺ Both taken. Updating the plan — this touches the C group, the assumptions
  table, and the spec.

❯ no refresh needed; still gone after reload - for D1, what does this           
  validation mean? How will we validate that all the data was deleted?          

⏺ Sharp catch — the D2 validation as written doesn't actually prove what it
  claims, and chasing it down surfaced an error in the spec.

  What D1's validation means — two distinct things:
  - "no refresh needed" → the row leaves the DOM without navigating (the
  prompt's "UI updates dynamically" requirement)
  - "still gone after reload" → it was persisted server-side. A reload
  re-fetches from the API, so if the row reappears, the delete only happened in
  the client cache and the server never saw it. That's the failure this catches.

  Your real question — how do we prove the status rows are gone — exposes a 
  hole. GET /api/jobs/<id>/statuses/ returning 404 proves almost nothing about
  the cascade: that route is nested under the job, so it 404s on the parent
  lookup. Orphaned JobStatus rows with a dangling job_id would produce a
  byte-identical 404. The assertion can't distinguish "cascaded" from "leaked".

  Proving no orphans remain needs direct DB access, which E2E doesn't have — so
  it belongs in a backend unit test (JobStatus.objects.filter(job_id=…) empty).
  I'm splitting D2 into the reachability half and the orphan half, and saying
  explicitly why the E2E half is insufficient alone.

  And a correction to SPEC.md: it claims cascade is "enforced at the DB level by
  the FK constraint… so it holds for raw SQL too." That's wrong. Django's
  on_delete=CASCADE is an ORM-level collector that issues its own DELETE
  statements; the Postgres constraint it emits has no ON DELETE CASCADE clause.
  A raw SQL DELETE FROM jobs_job would raise a foreign-key violation, not
  cascade.

      233  ### `DELETE /api/jobs/<id>/`
      234 -`204 No Content`. `on_delete=CASCADE` removes all `JobStatus`

      235 -constraint, not only in Django, so it holds for raw SQL too.  
      234 +`204 No Content`, and every `JobStatus` row for that job goes 
          +with it.                                                      
      235  
      236 +Worth being precise about *where* that is enforced, because it
          + is easy to assume more than is true.                         
      237 +Django's `on_delete=CASCADE` is an **ORM-level** rule: the del
          +ete collector loads the affected children and                 
      238 +issues its own `DELETE` statements. The foreign key Django emi
          +ts in Postgres carries no `ON DELETE CASCADE`                 
      239 +clause, so a raw `DELETE FROM jobs_job …` outside the ORM rais
          +es a foreign-key violation rather than                        
      240 +cascading.                                                    
      241 +                                                              
      242 +For a prototype where every write goes through the ORM that is
          + sufficient. It is called out here because                    
      243 +the difference matters the moment anything touches the databas
          +e directly — a bulk cleanup script, a                         
      244 +migration, psql. Making it true at the database level as well 
          +is one `RunSQL` migration                                     
      245 +(`ALTER TABLE … ADD CONSTRAINT … ON DELETE CASCADE`), and is t
          +he natural hardening step if this ever grew                   
      246 +beyond a prototype.                                           
      247 +                                                              
      248 +Testing follows the same split: E2E can only prove the history
          + is *unreachable* through the API (case D2),                  
      249 +because the nested `…/statuses/` route 404s on the parent look
          +up whether the children were deleted or                       
      250 +orphaned. Proving **no orphan rows remain** requires direct da
          +tabase access, so it is a backend unit test                   
      251 +(case D3).

⏺ Done and committed. Summary of what changed from your three points:

  State machine → permissive, but named. jobs/transitions.py will ship a
  permissive default next to the strict map it would enforce, with POLICY = 
  PERMISSIVE  # swap to STRICT. Your domain instinct is right and it's now
  written down as such — the reason permissive wins here is that the prompt
  excludes building a scheduler, so the dropdown is standing in for scheduler
  events rather than modelling a user workflow. Lock it down and you can't reach
  states to demo or test them, and the prompt explicitly asks for a control
  reaching "any of the defined states" — greyed-out options read as a bug. New
  case C10 unit-tests the strict map so it's functional code, not a decorative
  comment. Recorded as Q7.

  Same-status-twice kept but reframed exactly as you put it: an ordinary update,
  API-level test only, robustness against a double-submit or retry race rather
  than a product feature. Recorded as Q8.

  updated_at assertions added to C1, C4, and C8 — and this turned out to be the
  most valuable of the three, because the two timestamps are supposed to diverge
  and nothing was pinning that down. C8 now asserts a rename advances
  updated_at but leaves current_status_at untouched, which is the precise reason
  the guard compares against current_status_at.

  Your D1 question surfaced a real defect. D1's validation is two claims: the
  row leaves the DOM without a refresh (dynamic-update requirement), and it's
  still gone after a reload (proving the delete reached the server, not just the
  client cache).

  But your follow-up — how do we validate the status rows are gone — exposed
  that D2 never proved the cascade at all. GET /api/jobs/<id>/statuses/ is
  nested under the job, so it 404s on the parent lookup; orphaned rows would
  return a byte-identical 404. Now split into D2 (unreachable via API) and D3
  (backend unit: no orphan rows), since detecting orphans needs direct DB
  access.

  Chasing that also caught a factual error in SPEC.md: it claimed cascade was
  "enforced at the DB level… so it holds for raw SQL too." It isn't — Django's
  on_delete=CASCADE is an ORM-level collector, and the Postgres constraint it
  emits has no ON DELETE CASCADE clause. Raw SQL would hit a foreign-key
  violation instead. Corrected, with the one-line RunSQL hardening noted as what
  would make the original claim true.

  Plan is now 42 cases, 14 negative. Backend tests still 14/14. Two open items
  for you: the sign-off itself, and whether you want that RunSQL DB-level
  cascade added in slice 4 — I've left it out as prototype-appropriate, but it's
  cheap if you'd rather have the belt-and-braces.

✻ Baked for 13m 34s

❯ For the pagination - how about also testing deletion during pagination        

⏺ Good one — and it's a sharper test than creation, because deletion is where
  offset-based pagination visibly breaks. Let me verify how DRF's cursor
  actually behaves before I write the case, rather than asserting from memory.

  Ran 2 shell commands (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Confirmed, and it turned up something worth knowing. DRF 3.15.2's cursor
  builds its predicate from self.ordering[0] only — created_at — and handles
  rows sharing that value with an integer offset, not with the id tiebreaker.

  So my -id tiebreaker makes the SQL ORDER BY total (which is what stops
  nondeterministic ordering), but it does not enter the cursor predicate. Within
  a group of rows sharing an identical created_at, DRF falls back to
  offset-based paging — and that's precisely where a mid-walk deletion can skip
  a row. Your test idea targets the one weak spot in the scheme.

⏺ Skill(artifact-design)
  ⎿  Successfully loaded skill

⏺ Design plan before building:

  Palette — the chrome is near-monochrome cool slate; the only saturated colour
  on the page is job status. That's the core information-design decision: status
  pops because nothing competes with it. Interactive accent is a periwinkle
  #4b57c8 / #8f9cff chosen specifically because it reads as neither running-cyan
  nor completed-green nor failed-red.

  Type — no webfonts (CSP blocks CDNs, and a silent fallback is worse than a
  system stack). Character comes from role separation instead: job names in the
  UI sans, but every machine value — ids, timestamps, status labels — in
  uppercase mono with tabular numerals. Scheduler-output vernacular, drawn from
  the domain rather than applied to it.

  Layout — dense hairline-divided rows, not rounded cards with accent rails. A
  job queue is scanned, and rows are what scan well at the scale this thing
  claims to handle.

⏺ Mockup is live: ⧉ 
  https://claude.ai/code/artifact/4c5b63ba-db49-49f0-b561-d648d06cdde8

  The top half is a working prototype — search, filter, create with validation,
  status menu, expandable history, delete, load more. Flip Simulate API failure
  and the next status change or delete fails, so you can watch the optimistic
  badge roll back and read the actual error copy rather than take my word for
  it. Below that, a gallery pins each remaining state (loading, fetch failure,
  create rejected with the typed name preserved, delete dialog, end of list),
  each tagged with the test-plan case it satisfies.

  Three design decisions worth agreeing or overruling now, since they're
  expensive to reverse later:

  Status is the only saturated colour on the page. Chrome is near-monochrome
  slate; the periwinkle accent was picked specifically because it reads as
  neither running-cyan, completed-green, nor failed-red. Status pops because
  nothing competes with it. Colour is never the sole carrier either — every
  state has a distinct uppercase label.

  Rows, not cards. Hairline-divided rows with machine values (ids, timestamps,
  status labels) in tabular mono, job names in the UI sans. A job queue is
  scanned, not read, and this is the shape that stays scannable at the scale
  we're claiming to handle.

  The footer says "75 loaded", not "75 of 1,284." That's cursor pagination
  showing through — there's no total because computing one means a COUNT(*) per
  page load. If you want a total, it has to be an approximation on a separate
  cached endpoint, and I'd rather you make that call now than discover the
  absence in slice 10.

  Two things I'd flag as most likely to need your input: the history disclosure
  is currently a ▸ 3 events button per row — cheap, but it competes with the
  status control for attention. And delete has no undo, only a confirm dialog;
  an undo toast would be friendlier but needs a soft-delete or a client-side
  buffer, which is real scope.

  Committed as dc621bb; the file lives at docs/mockup/dashboard-mockup.html and
  republishing keeps the same URL.

  carry on with that while you look at this. Still outstanding from earlier: the
  test-plan sign-off, and whether you want the DB-level ON DELETE CASCADE
  hardening in slice 4.

✻ Crunched for 2m 23s

❯ In the change status - I thought we weren;t going to allow the backwards      
  status changes in the UI or did I misunderstand the design option? I think    
  it'll be better if the drop-down doesn't allow changing the status of a       
  completed job etc                                                             

⏺ You didn't misunderstand the intent — my option text said "all transitions
  allowed," so the "policy named and explicit" label oversold what was actually
  being chosen. Your call is clear now: strict in the dropdown, terminal states
  can't be edited.

  One consequence worth deciding before I implement, because it changes both the
  UI and the API. Strict means COMPLETED and FAILED become dead ends — once a
  job finishes, its status can never change again. That's honest domain
  modelling, but it also collides with the prompt's "update its status to any of
  the defined states," and an evaluator clicking a completed job finds every
  option greyed out with no way forward. That reads as broken unless there's a
  visible way out.

  An explicit Re-run action resolves it: the dropdown refuses backwards edits,
  but a separate labelled button moves a finished job to PENDING. No silent
  backwards transitions, every state still reachable, and it matches how real
  schedulers work — a re-run is a deliberate act, not a status edit.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · With strict transitions, how should a finished job behave? → Strict + 
     explicit Re-run (Recommended)

⏺ Implementing. One consequence to flag as I go: under a strict map, re-applying
  the same status (a double-click) would now 400 — which is hostile for what is
  almost always a double-submit. So same→same becomes an idempotent 200 no-op
  that appends nothing, rather than an error. That reverses Q8, and it follows
  from the workflow model you've picked: under permissive-observation-log
  framing a duplicate was information; under strict, it's just noise in the
  timeline.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

❯ Actually I think it onlhy makes sense to re-run a failed job, not a completed 
  one... how about we make that decision?                                       

⏺ Agreed, and it's a cleaner model — you retry a failure; you don't re-run a
  success. A completed job is done, and "run it again" is really a new job.
  Adjusting before I touch the mockup.

❯ Also does the search only search on the page, or across the whole database?   

⏺ Good question, and the honest answer differs between the mockup and the real
  thing.

  In the mockup it's client-side — there's no backend, so it filters the six
  loaded rows. That's a mockup artifact, not the design, and I'm labelling it as
  such so it doesn't get read as intent.

  In the real app it must be server-side across the whole table. Client-side
  search over one loaded page would be actively misleading at scale: you'd
  search "combustor", get nothing, and conclude it doesn't exist — when it's on
  page 400. Let me finish the mockup edits and record this properly.

  ⎿  Added 21 lines, removed 1 line
      178  Django REST Framework, `ModelViewSet`-shaped, JSON only.
      179  
      180  ### `GET /api/jobs/`
      181 -List jobs, newest first, **cursor-paginated**.                
      181 +List jobs, newest first, **cursor-paginated**. `status` and `s
          +earch` are both applied **server-side, across                 
      182 +the whole table** — never to the loaded page.                 
      183  
      184 +That distinction is the difference between a working feature a
          +nd a misleading one. Filtering a loaded page                  
      185 +client-side means searching "combustor" returns nothing when t
          +he match sits on page 400, and the user                       
      186 +concludes the job doesn't exist. At the scale this design targ
          +ets, client-side filtering is not a cheaper                   
      187 +version of search — it is a wrong answer delivered quickly.   
      188 +                                                              
      189 +The cost is an index question. `name ILIKE '%combustor%'` **ca
          +nnot use a btree index** — a leading wildcard                 
      190 +makes it a sequential scan, which is exactly the query shape t
          +hat falls over on a multi-million-row table.                  
      191 +The fix is a trigram index:                                   
      192 +                                                              
      193 +```sql                                                        
      194 +CREATE EXTENSION IF NOT EXISTS pg_trgm;                       
      195 +CREATE INDEX job_name_trgm_idx ON jobs_job USING gin (name gin
          +_trgm_ops);                                                   
      196  ```
      197 +                                                              
      198 +`pg_trgm` ships with the official `postgres:16` image, so this
          + is a migration (`CreateExtension` +                          
      199 +`AddIndex`), not a deployment prerequisite. Stated honestly: a
          + *highly unselective* search still has to sort                
      200 +a large candidate set, so trigram makes substring search viabl
          +e rather than free. Search results stay                       
      201 +cursor-paginated, so the result set is never materialized in f
          +ull.                                                          
      202 +                                                              
      203 +```                                                           
      204  GET /api/jobs/?status=RUNNING&search=fluid&cursor=<opaque>&pag
           e_size=25
      205  ```

❯ ht with                                                                       

⏺ Mockup republished at the same URL. Let me commit this.

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ You'd read the design option correctly — my label said "policy named and
  explicit" but the body said all transitions allowed, so the summary oversold
  it. Now reversed and enforced. Committed as b87e35e; mockup updated at the
  same URL.

  Transitions — PENDING → {RUNNING, FAILED}, RUNNING → {COMPLETED, FAILED}, both
  terminal states rejecting edits with a 400. The dropdown shows every state
  but disables the illegal ones with a "not allowed" note, so the rules are
  legible rather than invisible.

  Re-run only from FAILED, per your second message — a completed job shows
  "Completed — no further changes" next to Delete, rather than a dead button
  with no explanation. Re-running a completed job is a 400, not a hidden
  affordance.

  Two consequences worth your eye, since both are behaviour changes rather than
  mechanical follow-through:

  Same-status-twice flipped from 400 to an idempotent 200 no-op. A strict map
  has no self-edges, so a literal reading would reject a double-click as an
  invalid transition. That's hostile for what's nearly always a double-submit or
  a retried request. This reverses Q8 — under the permissive model a duplicate
  was an observation worth logging; under a workflow model it's timeline noise.

  Search is server-side across the whole table. In the mockup it filters loaded
  rows, because there's no backend — I've labelled that in the UI so it doesn't
  read as intent. The real one has to hit the database, otherwise searching
  "combustor" returns nothing when the match is on page 400, and the user
  concludes it doesn't exist. That's a wrong answer delivered quickly.

  The catch: name ILIKE '%term%' can't use a btree index — a leading wildcard
  forces a sequential scan, precisely the shape that dies on millions of rows.
  So it needs a pg_trgm GIN index. That ships with the postgres:16 image, so
  it's a migration rather than a deployment prerequisite. Added F10 to prove it:
  seed 250k, search for a name known to live far outside page one, expect the
  match — an assertion a page-local filter cannot pass.

  Plan is now 48 cases, 17 negative. Next up is slice 2 (POST /api/jobs/), with
  slices 3–4 now carrying the transition enforcement.
