  Name leads (it's what you scan for), status sits right where the eye lands
  before the controls, and Edit turns that badge into a select in place. Illegal
  transitions are disabled inside the select, so the state machine is visible
  at the point of use rather than hidden in a separate menu. That also let me
  delete the custom dropdown entirely in favour of a native <select> — fewer
  moving parts, and keyboard/screen-reader behaviour comes free.

  I drove it in Chrome rather than trusting the markup, which was worth doing —
  it confirmed the flows and caught three bugs:

  - A RUNNING job offers Completed/Failed with Pending disabled; picking
  Completed advanced the badge, grew the log 2→3, and flipped the row to
  terminal.
  - Re-run on the failed job returned it to Pending and grew the log 3→4,
  preserving the FAILED entry.
  - Bugs fixed: "1 events" pluralization; and .terminal-note/.scope-hint had
  been collateral damage when I removed the dead menu CSS, so "No further 
  changes" was rendering as body copy instead of a small label. The search hint
  also wrapped to three lines and now fits on one.

  Worth a look when you review: the three row variants are visible together in
  the screenshot — RUNNING gets Edit, COMPLETED gets "no further changes",
  FAILED gets Re-run. That asymmetry is the design you asked for, so it's the
  thing to sanity-check.

  Still open: the slice 5 sign-off (design + test plan), and whether you want
  the DB-level ON DELETE CASCADE in slice 4.

⏺ Done. Same URL: ⧉ 
  https://claude.ai/code/artifact/4c5b63ba-db49-49f0-b561-d648d06cdde8

  Aligned columns with ID (/var/folders/1t/2pzf8l8n1s91m08j0qjb7lw40000gn/T/clau
  de-chrome-screenshots-rjSX5U/screenshot-1784997529037-1.jpg)

  Numbering fixed — mockup is now slice 1.5, sitting where it was actually
  built. UI work reverts to 5–8, fault injection 9, scale 10, delivery 11; cold
  gates back to 0/4/7/11. The terminal list also had two competing "Slice 5"
  entries, which I've merged into one.

  ID has its own column, with a header row above the list — five unlabelled
  columns was asking you to infer what each one was. Every column is now
  fixed-width and left-aligned so IDs, event counts, and badges form clean
  vertical edges; actions is the single right-aligned column, so Delete lands in
  the same place whether the row shows Edit, Re-run, or "no further changes". I
  measured it in the browser rather than eyeballing: header and body edges
  match exactly, and all four columns align across every row.

  On the ID itself — BigAutoField already scales to 9.2 × 10¹⁸, so digits were
  never a capacity problem; the 4-digit values were just unrealistic seed data.
  Now 7 digits, which is what a system that's run a few million jobs actually
  looks like.

  The GUID question is worth separating from scale, though, because the real
  argument for it isn't capacity — it's enumeration. With sequential IDs and no
  auth (our A1 assumption), anyone can walk /api/jobs/1, /2, /3, and the ID
  itself leaks roughly how many jobs the platform has ever run. That's a genuine
  business-intelligence leak for a multi-tenant HPC product.

  The catch is that random UUIDv4 as a primary key hurts exactly the thing this
  design optimizes for: inserts scatter across the index instead of appending,
  causing page splits and fragmentation, and the index roughly doubles in size.
  UUIDv7 avoids that — time-ordered, so it keeps insert locality and is
  non-enumerable.

  My recommendation is to stay with bigint for this prototype and note UUIDv7 in
  the README as the hardening step, since the leak only matters once auth and
  tenancy exist. But it's a one-migration change now versus an awkward one later
  — say the word if you'd rather switch.

  What each slice added

  Slice 2 — POST. Creation routes through create_job() so the job and its first
  status event land in one transaction; a job can never exist with an empty log.
  Names are trimmed before the blank check, stored verbatim (unicode, emoji,
  and <script> round-trip unchanged), and aren't unique.

  Slice 3 — PATCH with the state machine. apply_status_change() takes the row
  lock before reading the status the decision is based on — otherwise two
  concurrent requests could both validate against a stale value. record_status()
  stays the low-level writer and enforces nothing, because job creation and the
  seed command legitimately write states the machine would reject as edits.
  Jobs now advertise allowed_transitions and can_retry, so the UI can disable
  illegal options rather than let you pick something the server will reject.

  Slice 4 — DELETE + history. The orphan check (D3) is a backend unit test, not
  E2E, for the reason you surfaced earlier: the nested statuses route 404s on
  the parent lookup, so orphaned rows would return a byte-identical 404. Only a
  direct query distinguishes them. I added a standing invariant too — every
  JobStatus points at a live job — which would catch a future delete path that
  bypassed the collector.

  Two harness fixes worth flagging

  Both were cases where a test tier wasn't doing what it claimed. Specs were
  only baked into the e2e image at build time, so adding one required a rebuild
  — the T1 "lightweight" tier wasn't lightweight. They're now mounted. And make 
  test-backend was testing a stale image; it now builds. Backend source is
  deliberately not mounted, so the image stays the artifact.

  CLAUDE.md is in place with the per-slice loop, the DockerHub constraint, and
  the settled decisions — so those persist across sessions without restating.

  Next up when you're back is slice 5, the first UI slice, building against the
  mockup you signed off. Nothing is blocked.

⏺ The whole backend is 565 lines across 10 files — small enough to read in one
  sitting. Here's the map.

  What's where

  backend/jobs/          ← the actual application (565 lines)
    models.py       82   Job, JobStatus, StatusType, indexes
    services.py     99   ★ the only writer of the projection
    transitions.py  77   ★ the state machine
    serializers.py  98   API shapes + validation
    views.py        73   the ViewSet, thin by design
    pagination.py   58   cursor pagination + why
    exceptions.py   60   one error shape for the whole API
    urls.py         12
    management/commands/seed_jobs.py   bulk seed for perf work
    tests/          35 unit tests

  e2e/tests/             52 Playwright specs (817 lines)
  frontend/src/          slice 0 shell only — the real UI starts at slice 5
  docs/                  SPEC · PLAN · TEST_PLAN · OPEN_QUESTIONS

  Everything else — config/, Dockerfiles, nginx.conf, Makefile,
  docker-compose.yml — is plumbing you can skim unless something breaks.

  Where to focus

  Read these three, in this order. They contain every decision worth arguing
  about:

  1. models.py — the shape of everything. 82 lines.
  2. services.py — record_status() and apply_status_change(). This is the heart:
  the only place current_status is ever written.
  3. transitions.py — the state machine as a data structure, not scattered ifs.

  Then serializers.py for one specific trick: status is a write-only field. It's
  not a column on Job — it's an instruction to append to the log. What comes
  back is current_status, the projection that append produced.

  views.py is deliberately boring — if logic ever accumulates there, it belongs
  in services.py.

  What current_status_at is for

  It's the timestamp of the JobStatus event that produced current_status. Three
  jobs, in descending order of how much they matter:

  1. It makes the projection self-describing. current_status = RUNNING alone
  doesn't tell you when it started running. To render "Running since 09:31"
  without it, you'd query the log per row — an N+1 across the whole list. This
  one's used every render.

  2. It's the guard in record_status() (services.py:44):

  if timestamp >= locked.current_status_at:
      locked.current_status = status_type
      locked.current_status_at = timestamp

  An event older than the projection gets appended to the log but doesn't move 
  the projection — so a late or replayed event can't drag a job backwards.

  3. It makes drift detectable. current_status_at == max(statuses.timestamp) is
  a cheap consistency check. Without it you can't tell a correct projection from
  a stale one without diffing the whole log — which matters because the
  projection is a cache, and caches need a repair path.

  Why not just use updated_at?

  This is the part worth understanding, because it looks like the obvious
  simplification and it's wrong. updated_at moves on every save — including a
  rename. Concretely:

  ┌───────┬─────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────┬────────────┐
  │ Time  │              Event              │ current_status_at │ updated_at │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ 10:00 │ job goes RUNNING                │ 10:00             │ 10:00      │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ 10:05 │ someone renames the job         │ 10:00             │ 10:05      │
  ├───────┼─────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────┼────────────┤
  │ 10:03 │ a delayed COMPLETED event       │ ?                 │ ?          │
  │       │ arrives                         │                   │            │
  └───────┴─────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────┴────────────┘

  Guarding on updated_at: is 10:03 >= 10:05? No — the completion is silently 
  dropped. A rename ate a real status change.

  Guarding on current_status_at: is 10:03 >= 10:00? Yes — applied correctly.

  That's test_a_rename_cannot_make_a_later_status_look_stale in
  test_services.py, and case C10 in the E2E suite.

  One honest caveat

  The guard is inert today. Timestamps are server-stamped and
  select_for_update() serializes writers, so events can't currently arrive out
  of order — condition #2 never fires in production as it stands. It costs one
  integer comparison and it's what makes the design correct the moment status
  events come from somewhere other than a person clicking a dropdown: a real
  scheduler emitting webhooks, a retried delivery, a backfill. Jobs #1 and #3
  above earn their keep right now regardless.

  Full reasoning is in docs/OPEN_QUESTIONS.md Q2 if you want the long version.

❯ For 1, can't you just use the updated_at field for when it got its latest     
  status? I don't understand why we need to do that                             

⏺ Fair challenge — let me test it rather than argue it. Creating a job, setting
  it RUNNING, then renaming it:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ There it is. Watch the third line:

  ┌─────────────────┬───────────────────┬──────────────────┐
  │                 │ current_status_at │    updated_at    │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ after create    │ 18:30:48          │ 18:30:48         │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ after → RUNNING │ 18:30:50          │ 18:30:50         │
  ├─────────────────┼───────────────────┼──────────────────┤
  │ after rename    │ 18:30:50          │ 18:30:53 ← moved │
  └─────────────────┴───────────────────┴──────────────────┘

  The job has been RUNNING since :50. updated_at now says :53, because it
  recorded the rename. "Running since 18:30:53" would just be wrong.

  The underlying reason

  The two fields answer different questions:

  - updated_at — "when did this row last change?"
  - current_status_at — "when did this job enter its current state?"

  They give the same answer only as long as nothing but a status change ever 
  touches the row. That holds right up until it doesn't.

  Today the thing that breaks it is rename. But the structural problem is that
  updated_at is bound to the row, not to the status — so any field added to Job
  later breaks it: a priority, an owner, a description, tags, a favourite flag.
  And it breaks silently. Nothing throws; the UI just quietly starts showing
  rename times as start times, and you find out from a bug report about a job
  that "restarted itself" when someone edited its description.

  So it's less "we need a second timestamp" and more "we need a timestamp that
  means what we're displaying." updated_at is a row-level audit field that
  happens to coincide with status changes in a system this small.

  You're right that it's contingent

  If we dropped rename, updated_at would be sufficient today — and the prompt
  only specifies PATCH as a status update, so rename isn't strictly required.
  That'd be a legitimate simplification: one fewer column, one fewer concept.

  I'd still keep current_status_at, for two reasons. It's 8 bytes a row, so the
  cost is noise. And it's what makes the projection verifiable —
  current_status_at == max(statuses.timestamp) is a cheap drift check, which
  matters because current_status is a cache, and a cache with no repair path is
  a liability at the scale we're targeting.

  But that's a judgment call, not a fact, and it's yours to make. Want me to
  drop rename and collapse to updated_at? It's a small change now — serializer,
  one migration, three tests — and considerably more annoying after the UI is
  built on it.

✻ Cogitated for 59s

❯ Oh I see - so if someone edits the name of the job, we still want to know how 
  long it was running. Got it                                                   

⏺ Exactly that. The rename is bookkeeping about the row; the status timestamp is
  a fact about the job's lifecycle, and editing a label shouldn't disturb it.

❯ Wby is status_type a string instead of an enum?                               
⏺ It is an enum in Python — StatusType subclasses models.TextChoices, which is
  str + enum.Enum. What's a string is the database column. Let me show you both
  halves:

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ So: enum in Python, varchar(16) in Postgres. In code you write
  StatusType.RUNNING, get autocomplete, and DRF rejects anything not in the enum
  (that's the 400 in case C7). The real question is why the column stores text
  rather than a smallint or a native Postgres ENUM.

  The three options

  Native Postgres ENUM — 4 bytes, enforced by the database itself. Rejected
  because it's a migration trap: adding a value means ALTER TYPE ... ADD VALUE,
  and you can't remove or reorder values at all. You'd be shipping a schema
  change that's effectively irreversible, for four states that might well grow
  to five.

  IntegerChoices → smallint — 2 bytes, the compact option. Rejected for two
  reasons:

  - The API speaks strings regardless — you'd never put {"status": 2} on the
  wire. So you'd map int↔string at the serializer boundary, and the database
  would no longer match the API. Every debugging session gains a translation
  step.
  - SELECT * FROM jobs_jobstatus returns status_type = 2. In psql, in a log
  line, in a dump, in whatever BI tool someone points at this later — you'd need
  the code open to read your own data.

  CharField + TextChoices — what's there. Readable at every layer, DB matches
  wire format exactly, and adding a state is a no-op migration: choices is
  Django-level metadata, so the column doesn't change.

  The honest cost

  This isn't free, and it cuts against the scale theme:

  ┌───────────────────────┬───────────┬───────────────────────┐
  │                       │  per row  │ 10M jobs + 30M events │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ varchar ("COMPLETED") │ ~10 bytes │ ~400 MB               │
  ├───────────────────────┼───────────┼───────────────────────┤
  │ smallint              │ 2 bytes   │ ~80 MB                │
  └───────────────────────┴───────────┴───────────────────────┘

  It also widens the composite index Job(current_status, created_at, id) by
  roughly 30%.

  I took that trade because a few hundred MB is cheap next to the operational
  cost of unreadable data, and because the shape of the query doesn't change —
  WHERE current_status = 'RUNNING' is an index seek either way, which is what
  actually matters at scale. Index width affects cache residency at the margin;
  index usability is what decides whether you scan 25 rows or 10 million.

  If it ever did matter, the migration path is open in a way the native-ENUM one
  isn't: add a smallint column, backfill, swap. That's the reasoning behind the
  docstring in models.py:22.

  justifies the feature: delete removes the record; cancel keeps it. A cancelled
  job still consumed compute time and is auditable history. Deleting it would

  On code review — one correction that helps you

  link, that's worth doing before you're relying on it.

  I haven't pushed, since publishing to a remote is your call. Want me to git 
  push -u origin main?

  What CANCELLED changed

  The state machine is now:

  PENDING   → RUNNING, FAILED, CANCELLED
  RUNNING   → COMPLETED, FAILED, CANCELLED
  COMPLETED → ∅                    (dead end — success)
  FAILED    → ∅  + re-run          (didn't finish)
  CANCELLED → ∅  + re-run          (didn't finish)

  Two details worth your eye at review:

  The migration is a genuine no-op. sqlmigrate emits (no-op) for both columns —
  adding a state cost zero schema change. That's the payoff of the CharField
  decision you asked about earlier; a native Postgres ENUM would have needed an
  irreversible ALTER TYPE.

  The retryable rule is now an invariant, not a list.
  test_every_retryable_state_is_terminal asserts the property rather than
  enumerating states, so adding a sixth status can't quietly create two ways out
  of one state.

  The practical fix for the cadence you described is a branch per touchpoint:

  main ──● (slice 4 reviewed + pushed)
          └──● slice-5-ui ──● ──●   ← review scope is exactly this

  That gives you three things at once: review scope that's precisely "since the
  last checkpoint", a PR you can run /review against, and a push that doesn't
  move main until you've approved it. It also matches how you described working
  — review and push at touchpoints, fast commits between.

  (backend complete), then cut slice-5-ui for the frontend work. I'd hold off

⏺ Review is in — 35 agents, 36 candidates, 33 verified, 3 refuted. I
  spot-checked all ten against current code first; the parallel session's
  changes didn't displace any of them.

  Two things worth pulling out of that list, because they're worse than their
  line numbers suggest:

  The status-update flow — the assignment's named critical flow — is broken in a
  real browser. Not degraded: the PATCH is never sent. It only triggers after
  someone expands a history panel, which is why every spec passes. That's the
  failure mode this project's whole test strategy is meant to prevent, and it
  slipped through because no spec combines two features that share a cache
  prefix. Worth a case in the plan, not just a fix.

  One is a threat to the gate itself. The loading skeleton shares .row with real
  rows, so spec 05's locator can match a skeleton on a cold machine — the exact
  warm-passes/cold-fails class T3 exists to catch, in the one command the
  graders run once.

  The rest are honest UI defects: telling users they have no jobs when the fetch
  failed, offering transitions the server will reject, an error message that
  can't be dismissed, and a history panel that silently truncates at 200 while
  reporting that as the total.

  Nothing here is a design problem — the architecture held up. These are
  integration seams: cache key prefixes, queryset scope, and CSS classes doing
  double duty as test selectors.

  Want me to fix them? I'd suggest all ten before slice 8, with two new specs —
  expand-history-then-change-status, and a detail route with a query param —
  since both defects exist precisely because no test crossed those boundaries.

※ recap: You're building the Rescale job dashboard take-home; slices 0–7 are done and the high-effort code review just returned ten verified findings, two of them serious. Next: say whether I should fix them before slice 8. (disable recaps in /config)

❯ I'm questioning the fault injection now - where would these faults be coming  
  from in teh grading sincde this app doesn;t actually run a job and it's all   
  local?                                                                        

⏺ Genuinely sharp question, and part of the answer is that some of it is
  theatre.

  What can't realistically happen in grading: network partitions, timeouts,
  backend-under-load. Browser and server are on the same machine over localhost.
  There's no scheduler, no third party, nothing to be flaky.

  What actually can, on a local single-machine run:

  - A stale tab. This one isn't hypothetical — it's the documented workflow.
  make test clears the jobs table before seeding. If a grader has the app open,
  plays with it, then runs make test in another terminal, every job on their
  screen ceases to exist. Their next click is a 404 on a row that was there a
  second ago.
  - Backend not ready. Open localhost:8080 during make up and the first fetch
  fails before the container is healthy.
  - Postgres restarting — docker compose restart db, or CONN_MAX_AGE handing out
  a recycled dead connection.
  - A genuine 500 from a bug in my code. Not simulated. That's what the uniform
  exception handler exists for.

  But the stronger argument doesn't depend on any of those being likely.

  The prompt requires error handling explicitly — "display a simple message if 
  fetching or updating fails." So the code exists whether or not the fault is
  probable. Fault injection isn't simulating production; it's the only way to
  execute a code path whose trigger you can't produce naturally. Untested error
  handling is almost always broken error handling.

  And the review just demonstrated that, concretely. Three of the ten findings
  are bugs in the error paths themselves — the app claiming "No jobs yet" during
  an outage, a validation error that can never be dismissed, one row's failure
  silently swallowing another's. All in code written to satisfy that
  requirement. Slice 9 is how you'd catch them.

  So my recommendation is to right-size rather than drop it — keep the specs,
  but stop describing them as simulated outages. The honest framing is: these 
  exercise the error-handling the brief asks for, using route interception 
  because a local stack won't fail on its own. Then add the two faults that are
  genuinely reachable — the stale-tab 404 and the backend-not-ready case — since
  those are worth more than a fourth flavour of injected 500.

  Want me to rescope slice 9 along those lines when I get to it, and note the
  reasoning in the README's testing section?

⏺ You're right, I overstated it. refetchOnWindowFocus is on, so switching back

  seeded data. To actually hit a 404 you'd have to click a stale row inside the
  window between focus firing and the refetch landing. That's a race, not a
  scenario, and I shouldn't have led with it.

  someone evaluating "implement basic error handling for API calls" would try.
  That's a real, reachable fault, and it's a much better justification than my
  stale-tab story.

❯ I do thinkn clicking on a link from a stale tab is valid too - that should be

⏺ No, you're right and I gave that up too easily. Let me check one thing that

  Ran 1 shell command (ctrl+o to expand)

⏺ Checked, and it's worse than I said — my concession was wrong on the
  mechanics.

  staleTime: 10_000 means refetchOnWindowFocus won't fire if the data is under 
  10 seconds old. Focus only triggers a refetch when the query is stale. So it
  isn't a millisecond race between focus and the refetch landing — there's a
  full 10-second window where the app deliberately declines to refetch and
  happily shows you rows that are gone.

  And the handling on that path is genuinely bad. onError restores the
  pre-mutation snapshot:

  onError: (_error, _variables, context) => {
    context?.snapshot.forEach(([key, data]) => queryClient.setQueryData(key,
  data));
  }

  So clicking a status change on a deleted job goes: optimistic update → 404 →
  rollback puts the deleted row back on screen → an error banner reading DRF's
  "No Job matches the given query." → and only when onSettled's refetch lands
  does the row finally disappear. The app resurrects a phantom row, blames the
  user with an opaque message, then silently removes it a moment later.

  That's not a race-condition curiosity — it's the ordinary behaviour of any
  cached SPA, and two tabs reach it with no timing involved at all.

  The fix is to treat 404 as its own case rather than a generic failure:

  - Don't roll back on 404. The optimistic state is wrong, but so is the
  snapshot — the row shouldn't exist at all. Drop it from the cache instead of
  restoring it.
  - Say what happened: "This job no longer exists — it was deleted." Not the
  ORM's phrasing.
  - A 404 on delete is arguably success. The user wanted it gone; it's gone.
  Reconcile silently rather than erroring.

  This is a real defect the automated review missed, because no spec acts on a
  job that's been deleted out from under the cache — which is a decent argument
  for your touchpoint reviews sitting alongside the tooling rather than being
  replaced by it.

  I'd add it to the slice 9 list as a proper case: delete a job via the API, 
  then act on the stale row in the browser — asserting the row disappears, the
  message names the cause, and nothing gets resurrected. That's reachable with
  two tabs and needs no injection at all, which makes it a better test than a
  fabricated 500.

  Want me to fold that into the fix pass along with the ten from the review?

      137 +  context?.snapshot.forEach(([key, data]) => queryClient.setQu
          +eryData<JobPages>(key, data));                                
      138 +}                                                             
      139 +```

      141 +`onSettled` already calls `invalidateQueries`, so skipping the
          + rollback is sufficient — the list                            
      142 +refetches and the row disappears because the server said so, w
          +hich is the whole point.                                      
      143 +

⏺ Added, marked "Required behaviour (decided — implement this, do not 
  re-derive)" so the other agent treats it as a decision rather than a
  suggestion to reason about.

  The instruction is the one you gave: on a 404, skip the rollback and let the
  refetch show current state. It's a three-line change, because onSettled
  already invalidates — so not restoring the snapshot is sufficient, and the row
  disappears because the server said so.

  Included the concrete onError snippet, plus three details that follow from the
  same rule:

  - The message names the cause, rather than surfacing the ORM's "No Job matches
  the given query."
  - A 404 on delete is success — the user wanted it gone, it's gone. Erroring
  there would be reporting a failure for an outcome that matched the intent.
  - The rule extends to the history panel: an expanded timeline whose job was
  deleted should close, not sit there showing a log for something that doesn't
  exist.

  Still gitignored (!!), path unchanged at .review/slice-7-code-review.md.

  Worth noting why your framing is better than mine: I'd said "drop the row from
  the cache," which means the client deciding what the server's state is.
  Refetching means it asks. Same visible outcome, but one of them can be wrong.

⏺ Done — one option, second: "2-digit" in frontend/src/lib/format.ts.

  07/25/2026, 09:14:03 UTC
  07/25/2026, 09:14:41 UTC

  Those two were indistinguishable before, which is the actual reason to do it:
  in the status history a job can go PENDING → RUNNING inside the same minute,
  and at minute resolution the entries read as simultaneous — the exact ordering
  question the log exists to answer.

  Two things I checked before touching it, given the other agent is in the test
  files:

  No spec asserts on rendered timestamp text, so nothing breaks. Both call sites
  (JobRow meta line, StatusTimeline entries) pick it up automatically since
  it's one shared formatter.

  Column widths are fine — the name column is minmax(0, 1fr), so the three extra
  characters flex rather than pushing the grid.
